ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e909	The issue of biotechnology law is ethically difficult, but also important. This law has remained unchanged for 16 years, and therefore the great majority of the Grand Duchy wants to modernise it. So the law has not been amended since 2004. At that time, John I. Alvheim stood in front to allow the PGD, which was absolutely necessary in order to give the family of Mehmet the opportunity to help their six-year-old child recover. It's been 16 years. Society has changed, and technology has become better, safer and more accurate, but the law has not changed as it does. I would therefore recommend the Committee's position, and each party will have to state its position on the matter. For us in the Progress Party, it has been important since the Mehmet case in 2004 to use today's medical methods to help families and individuals within ethical frameworks. The PGD offer for which Alvheim received a majority, we are further modernising today. We propose to make the PGD offer available in Norway, to wind up the denominators and to ensure that women and couples who are carriers of serious hereditary diseases and who are concerned that their future child will have a short and painful life, now through PGD can be allowed to have a healthy child. This is in line with John I. Alvheim's spirit, and I think he would be proud that the Progress Party made sure that his will was finally implemented. It’s high time – the last 16 years have the opportunity to give pregnant women better follow-up in pregnancy developed enormously, but the law has remained completely silent. The Progress Party will allow safer and better methods and follow-up in maternity care. Where women with high-risk pregnancies have had to carry out a water test to obtain information about their own pregnancy, a test which itself increases the risk of spontaneous abortion, a normal blood sample called NIPT can be taken today. NIPT is risk-free for both mother and child and can provide information to pregnant women in a gentle manner and early in pregnancy. This has been banned until now, but we will now allow it in Norway. The first ultrasound is a magical moment for coming parents – getting the opportunity to see the little man for the first time, getting a confirmation that it is alive, and want to write out some pictures that you can hang on the refrigerator and show up to friends and family. Early ultrasound is no enemy; it is a good one. We propose to do this as a fixed offer in public maternity care. In addition, for women carrying multiples, it is essential to know early on, so that you can get close and good follow-up. In some cases, it is also possible to provide the necessary health care to the foetus already in the womb. In the future, there will be even more treatments that can save children's lives in pregnancy, but then we will have to have a law that allows it, and women must be offered early ultrasound. The debate on foetal diagnostics is about whether to obtain information early or late, and whether to allow new and safe methods or to doubt old and risky methods. For all the women who feel that pregnancy is not compatible with life, we in the Progress Party believe that the most ethical thing is to let the woman get information about it early. Today we adopt a law that affects many people's lives, and precisely because the matter is difficult, it is also important. Technology is evolving, and it is time to use 2020 technologies rather than 2004 technologies. Where families are given information that the child carries the woman in the stomach, has a serious illness or disability, we as a society must stand up. Therefore, the Progress Party supports the government’s equivalence reform, which will give the family access to the coordinator already in pregnancy, and which can follow up and facilitate the family. We should always take care of families, even in a difficult situation. Anne Katrine has been involuntarily childless for eight years. She is brave who dares to stand up and tell about how it is. Getting cancer in the ovaries at the age of 21 and then experiencing that their eggs are being destroyed would have been tough for everyone to experience. Anne Katrine and her husband are not helped to have children because it is Anne Katrine who has challenges with fertility, and not her husband. Semen donation has been permitted in Norway for 90 years. It is generally accepted that if the man in the relationship has challenges with fertility, the couple may take advantage of sperm donation, but when the woman has challenges with fertility, egg donation is not allowed. There's really no fundamental difference. The party now proposes to allow egg donation in Norway, and we have spent a lot of time creating a framework for this that is ethical and professionally sound. An overall occupational environment and the majority of the population of Norway agree with us. In order to be allowed to donate eggs in Norway, a psychological assessment must be carried out first. The woman must be between 25 and 35 years of age and not have heritable diseases. Here it is important that the doctor make a thorough evaluation of all the ethical aspects that the woman must have thought carefully through before it is allowed to donate eggs. We expect the egg donation offer to be in place by 1 January 2021. We propose to legislate the child’s right to know the donor’s identity at the latest from the child’s age of 15. Parents get a statutory obligation to inform about it, and we set up a national register to keep track of it, so it's easy to get information. The Party considers that it is essential that the child be entitled to know his biological origin, whether egg donation or semen donation has been used. We separate layers with the Workers' Party and SV in relation to whom this offer is to be addressed. The Progress Party emphasises that donations should be an offer for those who want to have their own children. We therefore promote in the Committee's position on the proposal for a ban on embryo donation and dual donation. Embryo donation is where a family has assisted fertilization and had the children they themselves wanted. The fertilized eggs that were left over could in theory have been donated to other families. Children born after embryo donation will thus have genetic health care that grows up in several different families. That is what we believe in the Progress Party raises a number of ethical questions for the children, and we are against that. The point of progress is also against the possibility for couples or singles to receive both egg donation and semen donation. Children born after such a double donation will grow up in families where they have no genetic connection with their parents. The Progress Party therefore proposes that egg donation be offered only to couples – and only to couples who can provide half of the genetic material themselves. In this way, the child will have a genetic connection with at least one of his parents. I state once again that the child is given a legal right to know the donor's identity, and we hope that the government parties will therefore support our proposal for such an intrusion. I really hope that Norway, like most European countries, will decide to offer egg donation in Norway. For Anne Katrine and a number of women who are affected by serious illness and have had their eggs destroyed, this will mean an enormous amount – to be allowed to bring the child to their husband and fulfill the dream of establishing a family. Lonely shall also receive assisted fertilisation, and there are shared opinions on this. There are shared opinions in families, there are shared opinions in society, and there are also shared opinions in the different political parties. We have to respect that. When the Party of Advancement enters today in order to allow assisted fertilisation for solitude, it is under safe conditions that best suits the child. This is not a release. This is achieved in a way that is far more stringent in Norway than in countries with which it is natural to compare. In Norway, adoption of children has been one-off since the 1990s. Society has accepted that solitude can be good caregivers for children, but not for children with whom they have a genetic relationship. I think that's paradoxical. Individuals who want to have children must undergo an assessment of their suitability. The doctor shall assess here the financial situation of the woman, physical and mental health and social and family networks, and, if the State is to facilitate the provision of help to children by loners, the State may also make some demands. The Progress Party proposes that, in addition, a child-care certificate be issued. This shall reveal whether the woman is accused, intentional or convicted of serious crime affecting her care. This Child Care Certificate shall apply to all applicants for assisted fertilisation in Norway today. If serious crimes have been committed that affect the ability to be a good caregiver, they should also be rejected. Here we do not have a majority of the Workers' Party and the Center Party, but seek support from the government parties. No single woman will have access to egg donation. You have to have eggs yourself, so that the child has a genetic relationship with the parent they grow up with. We maintain the ban on surrogati. Children growing up shall have a statutory right to know the donor’s identity from the age of 15. The Progress Party therefore supports the fact that solitude can be assisted by conception, providing a strict framework to ensure that the child grows up with safe care personnel with whom they have a genetic relationship and where the child is also given a legal right to know the donor’s identity. I would like to take up with it the proposal that the Progress Party has made on the matter and look forward to a good debate.

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e987	This is a great wish in our people. According to the last study I've seen for Oslo, nine out of ten pregnant women already take early ultrasound because they want a confirmation of whether there is life, and how many lives there are. Early ultrasound is crucial for the detection of multiple conceptions, which will be important in order to be able to have a close and good follow-up, but it can also help to save lives. I record that the Christian People's Party has doubts that ultrasound should be given, but it should be given late in pregnancy. ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e994	So the chord change is over.
ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e1000	Today is a historic day on the Stortinget. I know I'm very excited. That's because what we're going to deal with today is so important. The proposals we have put forward will have an impact on the lives of women and families. Yes, because it's actually the very life we're discussing here today. The Labour Party has a long tradition of putting women’s health, maternity care and technological development on the agenda. The changes we are promoting here today are based on the shoulders of many women – and men – who have paved the way for us. In 1986, Gro Harlem Brundtland presented the famous women's government, and it was Social Minister Tove Strand Gerhardsen who was delighted to introduce ultrasound in week 18 as part of public maternity care. It's starting to be years ago. The countries around us have expanded their offer to pregnant women for many years. Norway is the most restrictive Nordic country in terms of biotechnology. Today, we are finally going to change that. I want to explain the four main issues that are at the heart of today’s treatment. Egg donation for solitude is about more women being given the opportunity to become mothers. Even I have four children, and I understand that there are many women who have a burning desire to bear their own children. Allowing egg donation is a matter of allowing couples with women who are struggling with fertility to receive the same help that families have been able to get with sperm donation for 90 years. I think it's so inwardly unfair that women should almost feel like lawbreakers when they need help for their infertility, when we have at the same time had the long tradition of helping men. For many years we have had safe, good methods of extracting eggs from women, and there are no longer rational reasons why women too should not be able to voluntarily donate eggs to others. The argument that women can be exploited in vulnerable situations will be met by strict restrictions on compensation. We also demand that the donor be unknown, so that you cannot be subjected to pressure from, for example, a sister or a friend. That women, for the rest, should be in a worse condition than men to choose whether to donate away sex cells, bottoms of attitudes that the Workers' Party does not support. Assisted fertilization for lonely is about the many hundreds of women who travel abroad every year to become pregnant. There may be many reasons why people choose to have children as lonely. There are probably several people who got the e-mail from the woman who had lived many years in a bad relationship. She wanted a child, but dared simply not to have it with her husband. Such stories impress me. When she finally got out of the relationship, she began to get it busy having children. She had not found love again and decided to carry out assisted fertilization abroad. Because that's how we women have a little less time than men. From the middle of the 30s, the fertility of women goes down quite quickly. That is why I am pleased that women now also have the opportunity to store their own unfertilised eggs. In the past, this has been limited to women who, for medical reasons, may become infertile. In addition, the own storage of eggs may reduce the need for egg donation. If you have stored your own eggs from a younger age, these eggs will be of better quality if you need assisted fertilization later in life. Being able to store their own eggs also completes the circle in terms of women's control of their own family planning. Women may inhibit pregnancy by using contraceptives. Women may terminate pregnancy by having an abortion. Now women can also promote pregnancy by giving them the opportunity to take care of their own young and healthy eggs. Then we've gone a step further and can move on to the pregnancy itself. Finally, the offer Tove Strand Gerhardsen introduced in 1986 should be extended and Norwegian women should have access to early ultrasound and foetal diagnostics. There are many reasons why we want to introduce this. I personally would like to make one point in particular: Each year, between 20 and 30 twins are affected by a life-threatening condition called twin-twin-transfusion syndrome. In such cases, nutrition, blood and foetal water are transferred from one twin to the other. It is dangerous for both of them, and in serious cases there is a risk of heart failure and brain damage. There are two whole school classes that can go down! Early ultrasound saves these children. In twin captivity, it is important to clarify early on whether the twins share morcoma. As a twin mother, I have experienced the safety of regular follow-up in a risky pregnancy. To me, early ultrasound was important. This security must be provided for all twin mothers. At the same time, I would like to stress that I understand that this debate is painful and demanding for many. To parents who have great kids with a developmental deviation or a chromosome variation, and who would never have been the kids before, I would say: When I fight for the right to know, it also applies to the right to not know. When I fight for the right to choose, it also applies to the right to bring forward a child with developmental deviation without feeling that one is a burden on society. We have to stand up for them! The individual woman and family will make the right choice for them and their lives. The task of society is to ensure that families are met with understanding, competence and services that can contribute to their child’s quality of life and life. And here we have a lot to go on. This must be the next big equality fight – an equality fight that is many years in overtime. The last main theme I want to highlight is vital research and treatment. It's not just ultrasound that has health benefits for mom and kids. With the amendments to the Biotechnology Act, we also ensure that foetuses can receive the leading medical treatment by lifting the ban on gene therapy of foetuses. Today, advanced surgery and other medical treatments are being carried out in the last trimester. For serious genetic diseases, genetic therapy in the foetus could be life-saving or reduce future disease, pain and suffering. How can you falsify the protection of unborn life when at the same time you vote against the access of foetuses to the best possible treatment? The Labour Party believes that we must provide this opportunity for our very smallest patients, and we do not understand that the government parties are voting against. The Cancer Association last week published the strong story of 15-year-old Simen, who, thanks to a clinical study, had access to ground-breaking treatment. The Simen had just been told that he was a cancer for the third time. However, after a few months of treatment, two out of three children participating in the study were non-cancerous. The Sim starts in high school until the fall. The Sim has life in front of it. The updates we propose related to gene therapy are about making Norway competitive in the fight for such studies. Because while technological developments in gene therapy have accelerated, the Norwegian Biotechnology Act in this area has remained unchanged. The Cancer Association fears that Norwegian patients will not have access to treatment that will save lives because Norway has a slower and more extensive bureaucracy than other countries. That's why we simplify and clean up the law. Finally, I want to say something about the process. The Solberg government presented the evaluation of the Biotechnology Act three years ago, and it was addressed by Stortinget in the spring of 2018. But they ordered the law changes were stopped when the Christian People's Party joined the government and got veto rights on all issues of biotechnology. When the Progress Party later left the government, we were given the opportunity to rectify this. Now that the primary position of the Right and Left is not expressed, I am pleased that there is a width of parties, from SV to the Advance Party, that promote these proposals together with the Workers' Party. It underlines how broadly anchored these issues really are, and it is a strength for the matter. I would also like to thank the representatives Åshild Bruun-Gundersen and Nicholas Wilkinson for their excellent cooperation. The case has been dealt with thoroughly in several rounds and over three years. There have been more consultations and the public debate has gone on even further. The unusual thing is, of course, that the law work is done here in Stortinget. But adopting such a law, as the Storting does now, is actually the legislator's privilege. That's our job. Moreover, from 20 February onwards and until the date of release, the majority of the parties have received legal assistance from the Ministry in order to ensure the quality of the amendments to the paragraphs and the fullness of the draft law. Once the political matter has been lost, the last straw is to criticise the process. I believe that is incorrect. What is going to happen here today is democracy in practice. Today we're really getting to know the importance of all power in this room. With that, I address the proposals of which the Workers' Party is part.

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e1115	Last year, the four non-socialist parties promoted an up-to-date biotechnology law. Much has happened in many areas since the law came into force, and there is a need to make changes in a number of areas. Only in this way can better treatment be provided for patients and professionals to develop better treatment methods. It was our government that agreed to change the Biotechnology Act after 16 years of standstill. The law is over-mature for change. We open up more research, better patient treatment and more efficient personalised medicine. Patients will now have better access to modern treatment, and we will simplify by removing unnecessary bureaucratic barriers. We bring about many important changes, such as removing the lower age limit for assisted fertilisation, extending the storage time for fertilised eggs and simplifying the rules for gene therapy. Biotechnology offers many opportunities for people in a society where there is room for everyone. At the same time, this is a matter that raises many demanding issues. Genetic and biotechnological knowledge and its practical use in healthcare have been an important part of the innovations in modern medicine. We are constantly getting more knowledge, and we are developing better and better methods that enable us to detect and treat diseases from the very beginning of life. It gives us great opportunities and presents us with difficult choices. The changes proposed by the government are based on the consensus of the Granavolden platform. The right is on concluded agreements and is therefore in line with what was proposed by the four non-socialist parties in government. Right is proud that, after no one else has succeeded in 16 years, we have brought about the modernisation of the Biotechnology Act with a thorough process and proper processor. This is, as I said, the first major amendment to the law since 2004. The absence of the red-green government resulted in delays in the legal work that the non-socialist majority of the population has taken on board. This modernisation opens up more research, better patient treatment and more efficient personalised medicine. We remove, as I said, the lower age limit for assisted fertilization, we extend the storage time for fertilised eggs, and we change the system to make it easier for a family to get the same donor for all the children. We ensure that insemination is not included in the government offer of three assisted reproduction trials. We impose an obligation on parents to inform the child if it has been conceived through assisted conception. We simplify the regulation of genetic testing in biotechnology research. Furthermore, we simplify and clean up the rules on gene therapy and exempt vaccines used in the national vaccine programme for the regulation of gene therapy in the Biotechnology Act. We lower the age limit for when the child has the right to know the identity of the sperm donor, from 18 to 15, and clarify the criminal liability by testing others, except for individuals who do so on behalf of and with the consent of the data. In addition, we remove the condition that gene therapy can only be used for the treatment of diseases that are considered serious. So here's a series of changes that benefit research. There was a lot of good in this law with which the new majority has worked. The rules on the use of stored fertilised eggs become simpler and the requirement for genetic guidance in predictive genetic testing is adjusted. The current requirement for the approval of diseases for which healthy people can be genetically tested is repealed and we clarify that the law applies only to projects where participants receive health care or are to receive individual feedback on the outcome of predictive genetic testing. The condition for gene therapy is clarified and demands for police and child care certificates are introduced to be approved for assisted fertilisation. Not all of these changes require changes in the law itself, but much of it is in the legal text itself. In addition, the right is included in the proposal to allow the willing of semen from deceased partners, as we believe it is well framed in the new legal text. For us, it doesn't seem logical to prohibit this as long as it opens up assisted fertilization to lonely. I think I'll come up with that later in the debate. They provide a good insight into how the process has progressed and must be considered as part of the process of this law. So to some of the points, and first the mitochondrial donation. It's the majority to open up treatment that leads children to get DNA from more than two people. There is insufficient knowledge of this today, including: the consequences it may have if donated mitochondria works poorly with the DNA of the mother’s cell nucleus. Mitochondria donation may also cause genetic changes in germ cells, and it is considered to be a scientific experiment. Norway cannot start with this, as it is contrary to international conventions. No country has fully succeeded, and the utility seems to be limited. Why, then, is it so important to press? The working party and SV will allow embryo donation and double donation. This creates children who have no genetic connection to their parents, and removes an important barrier to surrogati. Preimplantation diagnostics, PGD, are permitted in Norway today, but the application from the couples who want to use the method is assessed in a name of professionals. In the evaluation of the Biotechnology Act, the government stressed that removing the names could lead to pressure on the professional community to expand the offer and that a multidisciplinary composite approach ensures a broad evaluation of the applications. The right is behind this assessment. In early ultrasound: the Labour Party, the Advance Party and SV will give early ultrasound to all pregnant women as an offer in public health. There are probably arguments for that, but it is interesting to see the response from the professional team. This is very resource-intensive in a service that the Working Party in particular is busy pointing out is under pressure. At the latest in Bergen's Official Journal yesterday we could read that the Women's Clinic in Stavanger fears that this could have unintended consequences, where healthy pregnant women will be set up against women who do not have equal rights. The same applies to NIPT. NIPT is currently in use in Norway; nothing new is happening today. NIPT is authorised as a foetal diagnostic method in Norway for use in the identification of RhD-positive foetuses in RhD-negative pregnant women, for the detection of trisomi 13, 18 and 21 in foetuses, for the detection of serious hereditary disease and for the detection of severe genital disease. We've approved this in the last few years. The last approval was made on 2 March this year. In other words, the NIPT test is already part of foetal diagnostics, but for it to work in practice, we should not allow all healthy pregnant women to have the study. Then we're using the resources wrong. The NIPT requires specialised personnel and is a test that still has some uncertainty and scope for interpretation. ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e1202	The aim of the Biotechnology Act is to ensure that the medical use of biotechnology is exploited for the benefit of people in a society where there is room for all. This shall be done in accordance with the principle of respect for human dignity, human rights and personal integrity and without discrimination on the basis of heritage based on the ethical norms enshrined in our Western cultural heritage. For the Central Party, it is important that changes to the Biotechnology Act do not conflict with this overriding objective. We believe that the precautionary principle must be emphasised in issues where medical treatment may have adverse or irreversible consequences for our descendants. What is allowed in other countries cannot be decisive. Norwegian regulation of biotechnology needs to be assessed in the light of our own views on ethics, values and what is wanted national regulation and fair prioritisation of health resources. The government has proposed 22 amendments to the Biotechnology Act, and the Center Party supports them in all respects. The Labour Party, the Progress Party and the Socialist Left have tabled several new legislative proposals in the vote, in which the Central Party is essentially disunity. These have also not been out on the ears. I am pleased that a joint committee supports the proposals for decisions to ban genetic testing of children outside the healthcare system and that a person is given a distribution of the market for genetic self-testing with a view to regulating the law of the field. The centre party supports a new age limit for the right of children to donate information, is reduced to 15 years and an obligation to provide information to parents is introduced. We support the introduction of clearer and common guidelines for the assessment of the care of couples by assisted fertilisation, but we do not support demands for a police certificate to show whether a person seeking assisted fertilisation has been targeted, accused, instructed or convicted of multiple criminal offences, such as sexual crime, murder, drug crime and violent and criminality. But who should decide that previous convictions should not be entitled to health care in the event of infertility? What kind of crime is it that doesn't feel like being a parent? We believe that the requirement for police clearance goes too far. In addition, it can create false security. The centre party supports the storage of non-fertilised eggs and ovaries on a medical basis, but we strongly oppose the proposal from the majority to allow all healthy women to freeze eggs without a medical indication. This, we believe, could have unfortunate consequences, as well as commercialising reproduction and unwanted technology, and it could create pressure on young women to postpone pregnancy until later. The Working Party, Progress Party and SV will open for mitochondria donation in Norway. The center part thinks it's ethically problematic to open up for treatment where children get DNA from more than two people. We also think it is wrong to introduce a new method that is not now considered to be a medical defence agent, and where there is a lack of knowledge about the consequences. The proposal would be contrary to the biomedical convention to which Norway has committed itself. The Workers' Party, the Progress Party and the SV propose that we work towards a change in the Biomedicine Convention to allow mitochondria donation in Norway, indicates how far these parties are willing to go in order to apply the latest techniques in any event, even if they are contrary to international conventions in the field. The majority will allow egg donation in Norway. The centre party has confirmed that we do not want this. It opens for eggs to be moved from woman to woman. There is a more intrusive and more burdensome process than for donors of semen, so it cannot be equated. We believe that egg donation can lead to further materialization and technologyisation of reproduction, and that it is problematic against the Children's Convention and the rights of children have the greatest possible potential to know their parents. We also fear that an important barrier to surrogati will be removed if egg donation is allowed. Since the majority have refused to send the proposal on the phone, there are many questions about the device that will not now be heard. The working party and SV will allow the donation of both eggs and semen and the donation of ready-to-be fertilised eggs. That's not how the kids want a genetic connection to their parents. The centre party will, if appropriate, vote in favour of the proposals of the Progress Party here if not to allow this. The majority suggest assisted fertilization for single people. The center party has program confirmation that we believe that assisted fertilization should be reserved for couples. We have a special responsibility when, as a society, we create a framework around government provision that concerns children. It becomes more vulnerable when a child is growing up with only one caregiver and in this case without a father. In the case of assisted fertilisation for solitary, only women will be eligible, because surrogati is not allowed and will not be. If we introduce assisted conception for single people, break it with the principle of equality that men and women should not be discriminated against as caregivers. It also violates the principle that assisted fertilisation should deal with health care in case of infertility. But being single isn't a disease. The majority do not introduce any age limit, nor, and in principle, an 18-year-old single-bred woman can ask for assisted fertilization. I think there should be a limit on which the state should be involved in facilitating and financing. This is also a matter of priority in the health sector. The majority will terminate preimplantation diagnostics and transfer responsibility to the professional community. In this case, we are some with the government and the Biotechnology Council, which does not recommend this. The reason for this is that there will be more arbitrary outcomes of the applications and that the applications will not be as detailed as in the name of multidisciplinary composition. The majority suggest as early ultrasound as a routine offer to all healthy pregnant women to detect serious disease or harm to the foetus, as it says. Here, the plural part mixes ultrasound in normal ultrasonic pregnancy care as part of foetal diagnostics – it is essential. Yesterday I received 42,000 signatures against unnecessary foetal diagnostics. I have received many messages with criticism that these proposals are being put in place without any other explanation. The centre party believes there is no professional reason to carry out a mass investigation with early ultrasound of 50,000-60,000 healthy pregnant women every year. When the Knowledge Centre in 2012 summed up the research in this field, the conclusion was that there is no evidence of the health benefit of routine early ultrasound. There are no subsequent new knowledge summaries that provide the basis for a changed conclusion. Early ultrasound for all was also not recommended in the guidelines for maternity care that came in 2018, based on up-to-date medical knowledge on maternal and paediatric health. So what is the Workers' Party, the Progress Party and the SV know that this knowledge base hasn't figured out? To the argument about finding foetuses with a criminal offence: the acquisition of knowledge concluded that there is a great deal of uncertainty about diagnostic accuracy for the detection of serious structural abnormalities, which it is called, among other things. heart failure. Ethically, such a routine examination is required because a significant consequence of such a routine examination is to opt out of it – that is, an abortion may take place. This is also a matter of priorities. In BT yesterday it was said that proposals for early ultrasound for Haukeland University Hospitals would lead to 5,000 extra ultrasound examinations per year, six new medical settings and four new ultrasound machines at a price of 1.3 million. kr. I fear what consequences this will have for the rest of the healthcare system, and that a priority is for healthy pregnant women instead of those who actually have a need. The majority also want an NIPT test to be the primary test for foetal diagnostics, while allowing him to be performed for all healthy pregnant women. An article in the journal of the Norwegian Medical Association on 15 May this year states that NIPT is accurate for trisomi, but with a cost per additional case of trisomi of approximately 4.4 million. kr for one extra case detected. If NIPT is used as the primary test for all pregnant women, 271 out of 275 estimated cases of foetuses are detected, but one will also receive 230 false positive tests. It indicates that this is not the case for a routine test. I would like to take up with this the proposal of the Centre Party on the matter.

In Iceland, women are paid NOK 10 000 per egg donation. In Denmark, the payment is similar. When they increased compensation to this level in Denmark four years ago, they also received a double in the number of egg donors. No batches in this room want economically motivated egg donation, but what we're at risk is a class distinction in which the body is exposed to something that could be a health risk. It's not that egg donation becomes altruistic just because we write in a big decision that it should be – not if there's at the same time a payment that's a lot of money for people who can't afford it. That is why Red is voting against the proposal of the Labour Party, SV and the Progress Party today. We did the same thing in this room two years ago, because, like parts of the women's movement, we're worried about economically motivated egg donation, and because an informed choice requires sufficient information. As the Women’s Front points out, there are no long-term studies on the effects of the egg donation on the female body. The female front also says that it will be impossible to avoid a situation where some vulnerable and low-paid or poor women feel pressured to donate eggs because of the economy and the compensation planned. This is why we are proposing instead the following proposals: ‘The Government asks the Government to return to Stortinget with legislative proposals to allow egg donation from women undergoing IVF treatment and consenting to donation, with the same strict rules as for sperm donation. Donor shall be unknown and non-anonymous.’ That's what the Biotechnology Council went into in 2015. It was this SV suggested in Stortinget two years ago. This was the practice in Denmark from 1997 to 2003, when egg donation figures were relatively stable between 121 and 159 per year. On that basis, I take up Red's proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e1579	Medical progress gives us great opportunities and also presents us with difficult choices. The government's proposal for changes to the law will provide patients with better treatment and professionals with the opportunity to develop better treatment methods. It is high time that the Biotechnology Act was renewed. Much has happened in many areas since the law of the day came into force in 2003. Opportunities in these years have increased and increased, but many of the ethical challenges have also increased and increased. It is about exploiting biotechnology for the benefit of people in a society where there is room for everyone. This shall be done in accordance with principles of respect for human dignity, human rights and personal integrity and without discrimination on the basis of heritage based on ethical standards enshrined in our Western cultural heritage. This means that biotechnology must be regulated by law. It's too big a question to just leave to researchers and professionals on the one hand or to the individual's choice on the other, because both the researcher's choice and the individual's choice could have consequences for all humanity if the law doesn't set any limits. The bill that was promoted by the government has been made after a thorough process, even though it was urgent to update the law. In 2014 I gave evaluation assignments to both the Health Directorate and the Biotechnology Council, and in 2017 the government presented a global report. With the message, we brought the difficult issues in the biotechnology field to Stortinget. Not only did we present and discuss issues such as egg donation and assisted fertilisation for lonely but other important issues were presented, such as: storage of unfertilised eggs, diagnostics and simplification of the law to provide better research. Questions that had lasted a long time, we highlighted with the latest information on professional development and arguments for and against, and several proposals were made. In December last year, the government presented a total of 16 proposals for amendments to the Biotechnology Act for the Stortinget. These are changes that will give patients access to better treatment and allow professionals to develop better treatment methods. Among other things, the rules for genetic research will be modernised. Genetic research gives us more knowledge of why we get sick, and better treatment when we get sick. Now we're making it easier to do this kind of research. Gene therapy is increasingly giving us opportunities to treat disease by repairing genes or replacing missing genes. These are the possibilities we're going to use more. Gene therapy should now be available for the treatment of all types of diseases, not only those characterised as serious. Today's biotechnology law has a rigid regulation of genetic research. Genetic studies are an important tool in disease treatment and disease risk monitoring. They make it possible to tailor the treatment for the individual patient. Amendments to the laws should facilitate the use of genetic studies in patient treatment. Today's Biotechnology Act requires the destruction of fertilised eggs after five years. It is too strict and has led to pressure on families who have been dependent on assisted conception to have children. We are now lifting the five-year limit for the storage of fertilised eggs and giving the establishments an obligation to keep fertilised eggs until the age of 46. The current Biotechnology Act only allows women to store eggs and ova during treatment that reduces fertility. Women who lose fertility early for other reasons have not had this possibility. Now more women are to be given the opportunity to store unfertilised eggs and ovaries. The position of children shall be strengthened. Parents are required to inform children that they have been given semen donation. In addition, children are entitled to know the donor’s identity when they reach the age of 15 years — today the age limit is 18 years. Much argues that it is positive for the child and the family that the parents are open to the child that it has been produced by means of semen donation. Unintentional detection can be dramatic, psychologically traumatic and devastating for the trust of parents. Many children do not have the right to donor information because their parents do not say that they have been made by donation. When we help children into the world, we also have to do what we can to make them feel safe. Couples seeking assisted conception shall be required to provide a child-care certificate stating whether they are intended, accused or convicted of serious criminal offences, such as: sexual crimes, gross violence and murder. We also set an upper age limit of 46 years for assisted fertilisation to prevent treatment being offered to increasingly elderly women who are at high risk of complications. The biggest and most extensive changes in the law that are being made today, to which I have now explained, and to which everyone has a majority today, are the result of the cooperation between the Right, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left. The debate and vote today will show whether there will also be a majority of the most controversial and controversial changes that have often overshadowed – and even today overshadows – the rest of the debate on biotechnology. But I do not see the idea that in these questions too, there is still an important distinction between the Progress Party on the one hand and the Labour Party on the other. This is an important threshold that is essential for the development of legalising surrogati in Norway. That the Progress Party says no to the donation of fertilized eggs and demands that there should be a biological bond to at least one of the parents, is a very important limit to surrogati to which I also hope the majority of the Stortinge join today. The methods used for foetal diagnostics are not regulated by law. I perceive that the majority actually believe that all pregnant women in Norway are now to be offered foetal diagnostics without any indication or increased risk. It will require that women be given good information both in advance of the conduct of such a study and subsequently when the results are to be disseminated. There is a re-priority of the use of health professionals from work with patients in need of follow-up to the diagnostics of healthy pregnant women without any indication of any need to do so. The only purpose you can see is to find as many fetuses as possible that are different. I believe that this is contrary to the purpose of the law and is also contrary to how we otherwise prioritise health care resources. This is a proposal that has not been sufficiently clarified. We don't know the consequences for health care. We don't know how that can be done. And neither do we know the consequences of these proposals for the offer to other patients. This is not part of the law itself, because it is not regulated in the law. It is a Roman number proposal that the majority have put forward, and it violates the way we introduce new methods and diagnostic methods in healthcare. It breaks with the priority stitching, and it breaks with the system of new methods.

ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e2291	I would like to say a few words about a matter which has disappeared a little in the debate today, but which is not less important for that reason. This applies to genetic self-testing of children outside the healthcare system. There is today a market for genetic self-testing. It is usually run by large international companies with head offices abroad. You can easily take a water stick in your mouth and send in your own genes for testing. Here you can identify ethnicity and kinship, or you can identify the risk of a future disease. There is reason to be concerned about the possibility of commercialising and researching biological material that has been collected in this way. And what about the actual test? How reliable is it? Those taking it are not necessarily surrounded by competent health professionals who can interpret the results for them. A positive response can cause absolutely unnecessary health anxiety, and a negative response can provide false security. The market for genetic self-testing is growing. There's certainly not a general ban is the right way to go, but I think there's a reason to look at how we regulate this market, and at least provide for a more informed public debate on this, so that more people become aware of the consequences it might have to submit such a test. And what about children? There is currently no prohibition on transmitting their own child's DNA. This opens up serious interference with the integrity of children. It's not sure this is information they actually want. People have a different view of how much they want to know about their own future health, and with these children they are deprived of the right to choose this for themselves at a later stage. I am therefore pleased that a unanimous committee has agreed to ban genetic self-testing of children. So it's not given that it's the biotechnology law that's the right place to legislate this. That is why it is also a point that the Committee is now asking the government to come back to Stortinget with a legislative proposal. Maybe it's the Children's Act, or maybe there's another law where this is best suited. The most important thing is that the integrity of children and the right to privacy are safeguarded.

He was also very Christian and concerned about value issues, but he was one of them at this house at the time that was most concerned with the possibilities offered by the Biotechnology Act. I think that's important. He was concerned about value issues, he was concerned about the Christian values, but he actually believed that it was about helping all people in the best possible way using new technology. I believe that biotechnology offers many opportunities, and I believe that Mehmet was one of the examples that showed how important it was to take advantage of the possibilities it offers. With such a person in mind, as one of the people you've followed, and who you think had good values and good attitudes, but also great respect for using the opportunities offered by biotechnology, I'm going to vote in line with what the Progress Party has decided. But I fully agree that there are many dilemmas and many challenges. I have finally chosen to end there. I'm concerned that we're going to have a society where there's room for everyone, and I think you can still have that with what we're doing now. I have great respect for people and parents who can make the right and good choices for their future.

I The government is asking the government to ensure that, in the future, the biotechnology law can be evaluated more dynamically and continuously in order to keep up with the rapid development of biotechnology. II The government asks the government to amend the Medical Birth Register Regulation with a view to recording all treatments with assisted fertilisation in the Medical birth register, regardless of outcomes. III The government is asked to work to amend or understand the Biomedical Convention in order to ensure that mitochondrial donation can be permitted in Norway when the method is safe and professionally sound. IV The size asks the government to come back to Stortinget with an investigation into the market for genetic self-testing, which will provide a basis for regulatory regulation of this market. The investigation must take into account, inter alia, the protection of privacy, the right to own health data and the health consequences of misleading test results. VT The government is asking for legislative proposals to ensure that genetic testing of children outside the healthcare system is banned. VI The size asks the government to develop guidelines for compensation for the donation of germ cells, reflecting the load and time spent by the donor. Compensation is set at a moderate level, which does not drive up the overall level of compensation in Nordic countries. Documented travel expenses are also covered. VII The government is asking the government to ensure that employers are exempted from the obligation to cover sick pay for women who want to donate eggs. VIII The government is asked to design a priority guide for the donation of eggs, which largely harmonises with the priority guide for the donation of semen. IX The government is asked to provide a review of pre-implantation diagnostics and diagnostics conditions and guidelines so that they can be viewed in a coherent manner and provide a coherent and coherent range of services for women and families with a particular risk of serious disease or harm to the foetus. The review shall focus on suffering and loss of quality of life through relief and life-prolonging treatment in the review of the conditions for obtaining PGD. The Government is requested to propose the necessary legislative changes as a result of this review. X The government asks the government to ensure that ultrasound with supplementary studies capable of detecting serious disease or harm to the foetus becomes an offer to all women in the first trimester through public maternity care. XI The government is asked to ensure that the age limit for offering foetal diagnostics is lowered from women > 38 years of age by the future to women > 35 years of age by the future. XII The government asks the government to ensure that non-invasive prenatal tests are offered to all women who are subject to foetal diagnostics, or in the case of early ultrasound findings with supplementary studies, regardless of the age of the woman. [[NIPT]] XIII The government is asked to ensure that non-invasive prenatal est is allowed in Norway for all pregnant women. [[NIPT]] XIV The government is asking the government to ensure that Norway is actively working in international contexts to combat the exploitation of women in the international surrogati industry. XV The government is asked to incorporate the economic impact of the changes in the Biotechnology Act into the State budget for 2021.

This means that the base crew is higher than the minimum level required to provide a defensive service, so it is not always necessary to install temporary workers in case of absence. Increased basic staffing makes it easier to offer whole and fixed options, and so it is part of the solution to the challenges of recruiting municipalities. Increases in basic staff also reduce the workload on those added and may contribute to lower sick leave. Reduced temporary use also means that users get fewer people to stay with, leaders will spend less time managing rents, and it's cheaper because the hourly cost for temporary workers is relatively high.
