ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e4543	Let me first thank the Presidentship of the Stortinge for the opportunity to make a statement on the situation in the field of child protection. My ambition is to give a nuanced and comprehensive picture of the situation and at the same time describe how the government meets the documented challenges in the service. I also want to take into account the coronary situation, with particular emphasis on the consequences it has had for vulnerable children. Child protection depends on having the trust and reputation of society to reach children and parents in need of help. A number of demonstrations and media reviews have, in addition to the cases dealt with in the European Court of Human Rights, the EMD, contributed to creating a unilateral negative impression of the service. This is the background for me asking to make this statement. I fear that persistent negative attention could have more unfortunate consequences – for children and families in need of child protection, for the reputation of the service, for the confidence of the population in child protection, for the recruitment of employees – and also pass on reputational challenges to Norway internationally. In the country's small and large child protection services, the skilled professionals work with warm hearts and wise heads. These are employees who are very much in need of all children and young people to have a safe and well-growth. A unilaterally negative attention to the service is particularly unfair to it. Above all, however, the vulnerable and vulnerable children are affected. For these children, child protection will often be the main safety net. It's been a special spring for all of us. The anti-infectious measures resulting from covid-19 have led to major changes in everyone's daily lives. Uncertainty, shutdown and isolation are something that few of us live well over time. This spring has been particularly stressful for those of us who already was in a difficult situation. In order to improve the situation for vulnerable children and young people, the government has implemented a number of measures, and we also propose a number of measures in the revised budget. In addition to large allocations for ordinary services, we have proposed measures of more than 400 million. kr on the responsibilities of different departments. The proposals include, inter alia: measures in the field of education, health and child protection. In order to provide vulnerable children, young people and families in this crisis, child protection services have had to change in a short time. My impression is that many people have done this in a good way. During crisis management, many child protection services have received fewer concerns than usual. It is important for me to say that it is never wrong to report a concern for a child. That is why I am pleased that we have now opened the National Portal of Concern messages, where everyone can report their concerns digitally. We have also arranged for the alarm phone to be more easily accessible to children and young people. A chat function has been developed, which lowers the threshold for children and young people to make contact. Both the alarm phone and the chat function have extended the opening time and are now open 24 hours. In the revision, the government proposes to strengthen the allocation so that the alarm phone can maintain the 24-hour operation out of the year. In spring 2020, the government established a coordination group at the directorate level to ensure both the attention and knowledge of vulnerable children and young people during this critical period. The group, which regularly reports to me, has estimated that about 20 pst. by children and young people in Norway are considered to be particularly vulnerable. Early in crisis management, it was clear that child protection has social-critical tasks, and this has been important in maintaining the provision of services. We were also early in providing information to all municipalities on the tasks that child protection needs to prioritise in capacity challenges. It also provided clear advice that municipalities should not lead to the avoidance of physical encounters with children and families by child-restraint workers. A Child Protection Risk Assessment Guide has also been developed. The guidelines have been developed in consultation with the Institute for Public Health. In order to prevent child protection and social affairs from becoming a bottleneck in the system, a temporary law has been adopted which provides for greater flexibility in the settlement of cases and the possibility of dealing with cases without physical presence. Although surveys have shown that child protection has largely performed the most important tasks throughout the period, many have experienced child protection services as less accessible. The most recent monitoring of the state of child protection was carried out at the end of April. This suggests that municipal child protection has largely adapted to the new situation and found good solutions for carrying out its statutory tasks. They succeed, among other things: improving the maintenance of relief measures and the implementation of association, but there are still fewer concerns than it normally does at this time of year. Recently, I met with the Swedish Children's Protection Association and other user organisations in the field of child protection. The organisations described major differences in the way children in child protection have had during the course of coronary crisis. Many children living in institutions have had new rules and procedures to deal with. Among other things, they have had to have homework at the institution. For me, it has always been important that the schools and kindergartens opened up so quickly the situation allowed. Children did not go to school, kindergarten, football training, permanent school, dentist or health centre. As a result, many of those who usually report concerns about child protection did not do so during this period. I am pleased that the schools and kindergartens have now reopened, and that we can all gradually take our daily lives back. It's going to be a different summer, but I hope and believe that the services for vulnerable children and young people will soon be back to full. I am concerned that children and parents will get the right help early enough. My starting point is that parents are the most important caregivers in children's lives. Small and large events, however, can put family life to the test and challenge parents' ability and ability to provide children with the care they need. Then society will support, guide and relieve. Our policy is to strengthen prevention measures so that most possible children can grow up in their families. At the same time, there must never be any doubt that we take the best interests of the child into account. Norway has been, and will continue to be, a leading country in protecting the rights of children and in providing them with real content. We can be proud that we view children as independent individuals, with our own rights and the right to be heard. We must stand firm that the best of the children is the leading principle in all work for a childhood without failure. The right of children to safety and protection shall always be a priority. Child protection is one of the most important and perhaps the most complex welfare services we have. The children and families who receive help from child protection are among the most vulnerable in our society. This is precisely why it is so important that matters are handled properly and that children and families in the field of child protection receive good and proper help. The number of children and young people receiving child protection has increased in recent years. From 2014 to 2018, the increase was about 5 pst. During 2018, about 55,000 children received child protection assistance. Most of these children received relief while living at home. Most children and young people who cannot live at home move to a foster home. Some live in institutions or dwellings with follow-up. A total of 15,140 children and young people lived in foster homes or institutions at the end of 2018. In my opinion, Norway has good child protection. Developments in recent years have been positive. Several children receive help, and reports from municipalities indicate better compliance with legal requirements. At the same time, there are still considerable differences in quality between municipalities. Monitoring and research reports have highlighted quality and structural challenges, including: in the form of weaknesses in the interaction between different services, both in individual municipalities and between levels of government. It is shown, among others: to enable the assistance provided to be perceived as fragmented and to be too much adapted to the needs of children and families. Substantial challenges have also been identified in parts of the provision of institutions. This is serious. Institutions shall help the most vulnerable children, where the height of fall is greatest. Still, sometimes we get to grips with the children who have the greatest help needs, often at the intersection of drugs, crime and mental health. We need to learn from mistakes, whether it's through individual cases before the court or through detailed investigations of different areas. We know that there are quality challenges in several municipalities, and that there are too many variations in the help the children get. This applies both to the assistance provided to children living at home and to those living in foster homes or institutions. We also know that the services to provide vulnerable children and young people do not always cooperate well enough, either with each other or with those in need of help. The child protection service has a special responsibility to help children obtain the full assistance they need. They shall cooperate with the other auxiliary equipment and contribute to the provision of necessary assistance and follow-up from other services to children. Children’s issues have become a burden on Norway’s reputation in a number of countries. The campaigns are carried out in social media and on the Internet, and are often marked by hateful and gross accusations against Norwegian authorities. We work closely with the U.D. and the embassies to respond to such campaigns and to provide accurate information on child protection. Norway participates in several international networks and organisations where work on disinformation and false news is on the agenda. Norway also has close cooperation with the Nordic countries and other European countries, including Norway. through the Council of Europe and the OSCE. The EMD has found infringement of the European Convention on Human Rights, the EMC, in seven child protection judgments against Norway. I will address these judgments initially against Norway. I will then report on ongoing changes in the service that will strengthen children and parents' legal certainty and improve the quality of service provision. Since 2015, the EMD has dealt with 37 issues that all raise questions about child protection measures that have infringed the right to family life of the European Convention on Human Rights, EMC, Article 8. Out of nine judges so far, the EMD believed that the right to family life was violated in seven cases. One of the convictions is passed in the EMD Grand Chamber. Three of the convictions are not enforceable. In all cases where the EMD has found that there has been an infringement, it has been justified by weaknesses in the specific decision-making basis or justification of the child protection system or the courts. EMD has not found that the rules of the Children’s Protection Act on specific measures such as these are contrary to the EMK. The Court of Justice has also made it clear that the Norwegian legal security guarantees are reassuring. On the other hand, the Court of Justice has pointed out that the reasons for the choice of child protection measures have not been sufficient and that insufficient provision has been made for the child to be reunited with his or her parents. I look seriously at the judgments of the EMD, and we're working to follow up the learning points from these. An important measure is to disseminate knowledge of the judgements of the child protection sector. This applies to the municipalities, the county councillors and the county councillors. A good knowledge of these learning points will help to guide Norwegian child protection practices in the future. I have contacted various actors in the field of child protection about this. Bufdir cooperates with the statesmen to get the knowledge out to the municipalities. I also know that the subjects for child protection and social affairs are concerned with following up the EMD judgments. In March, the Norwegian Supreme Court assessed whether the Norwegian legal status was in accordance with the EMK, the Children’s Convention and the Constitution. The Supreme Court has also concluded that the rules of the Child Protection Act as such are in conformity with the ECHR. The Supreme Court provides important guidance on how the Child Protection Act is to be practised within the framework of the EMC and points to the need for adjustments in Norwegian child protection practices. Among other things, the Supreme Court stressed that association must be determined on the basis of the circumstances of the individual case. The clarifications of the Supreme Court will also be communicated to the municipalities. A number of measures are therefore being taken to contribute to the proper enforcement of the judgments of the EMD and the Supreme Court, both of the Department, the Directorates and the denominators. These measures will help us improve Norwegian child protection for both children and parents. Although the judgments of the EMD provide important learning points, the government has decided to request retreatment in the EMD’s Grand Chamber of the last two judgments issued by the EMD against Norway. The EMD has also decided to review another Norwegian child protection case, which was decided in the Chamber in December 2019. Child protection issues involve difficult balances where the consideration of biological parents needs to be balanced against what is best for the child. It is essential in Norwegian law that a care takeover is temporary and that the aim is to reunite the child with its biological parents in the long term. But child protection must at all times consider what is best for the child, and that is what we believe is crucial. The principle of the best interests of the child is strong in Norwegian law and is a recognised principle also internationally. This will mean that in some cases we need to place greater emphasis on the child's own right to health and development protection and less emphasis on preserving the unity of the biological family. The threshold for large chamber treatment in the EMD is high. However, I believe it is important that we make use of the opportunities offered by the EMK to help ensure that the child's best performance is adequately passed on to the EMD, in line with the Convention on the Rights of the Child and the Norwegian legal tradition. The best interests of the child must be the fundamental importance of child protection matters, and we will ask the EMD to clarify the assessments that need to be made when emphasising the best interests of the child. I am concerned that the legislation should be accessible to users and a good tool for child protection services. One of the first things I did when I became Minister for Child and Family Affairs was to send a proposal for a new Child Protection Act at the hearing. The new law shall be simpler, more accessible and better adapted to today's society. The aim is for the new Child Protection Act to lead to better professional work and increased legal certainty for children and parents. It is a fundamental principle that the linguistic, cultural and religious background of children should be safeguarded in the field of child protection. This applies to all with a minority background in Norway. In the new Child Protection Act, we propose to legislate that child protection should take this into account in their work. The best thing for the child is the overall consideration of child protection. The proposal for a new law is based on the changes from 2018, when the Children's Protection Act became a right law. The proposal for a new law places the child’s needs in the centre and further facilitates the child’s being able to explain himself as freely as possible in his/her case. We will build on the resources that exist around the child, and facilitate the involvement of the child's family and network. This is important both in order to be able to put in place measures that are well adapted to the needs of individual children and families, and in order to ensure important relationships for the child if there is a need for out-of-home action. The new law will contribute to an increased focus on prevention and early intervention. In this way, the law will provide a better professional tool for child protection services and other child protection actors. This could help to improve the quality of child protection and strengthen the rights of the children who need it most. The proposal for the new Child Protection Act was being consulted in 2019. I am pleased that a large majority of the hearing bodies give overall support to the proposal for a new law. However, the hearing bodies do have a number of recordings. Several call for more mention of human rights considerations. In addition, the new Children’s Protection Act will be presented at the same time as there are still several issues for treatment in the EMD. In view of this, it is particularly important that the new law contains good human rights assessments. I want the proposal for a new law to provide good guidance on how the Child Protection Act is to be practised within the framework of human rights obligations. I aim to present proposals for a new Children's Protection Act in the spring of 2021. Although a new Children's Protection Act will be presented next year, I have in Prop. 84 L, which was presented on 24 April of this year, has already made proposals for some legislative changes. We have had successful attempts in the subjects with the conversation process, and I propose to introduce it as a permanent arrangement. This is a form of procedure in which the head of staff invites the parties to the case for discussions, among other things. to examine whether agreement can be reached on a solution best suited to the child. The use of the conversation process will contribute to less coercion and may also lower the level of conflict between the child protection service and the parents. I hope this will create greater security for the child and less burdensome legal proceedings. In the same proposition, I suggest raising the age limit for post- protection, so that fewer people who have experienced care failure are excluded as young adults. In addition, we will ensure that the quality and staffing situation of each municipality is regularly assessed. It shall become more visible which municipalities do not give sufficient priority to the protection of children. I propose to introduce a requirement for annual status reporting from the Child Protection Service to the local government. This means that the municipality must regularly assess whether the quality and staffing of the child protection service is good enough. Municipalities are responsible for a number of offers and services for children and young people, including child protection. Stortinget addressed in 2017 Prop. 73 L for 2016-2017 on the Child Protection Reform. The aim of the reform is to strengthen early intervention and prevention, so that more children can grow up in their families and their communities. The reform of child protection is a quality reform. It shall enhance the quality of services provided to children and families through changes in the division of responsibilities between country and municipality, provide a clearer regulation of private actors, as well as various digitisation and competence measures. The liability changes shall take effect in 2022. Municipalities will have greater financial responsibility for child protection than they have today. This will be compensated for by an increase in free revenues. In this way, municipalities will have a greater economic space to build up good relief measures. Increased funding responsibilities through the use of state child protection measures, such as institutions, will strengthen the incentives of municipalities to give priority to early intervention and prevention. We are already well-informed on quality raising measures. These are based on the fact that employees are the primary resource of child protection. If we are to succeed in meeting the challenges, those who work in the services must have good working conditions. They must experience confidence and support in their professional activity. They must have access to modern digital tools and the necessary skills to provide timely and timely assistance. The opposition has repeatedly pointed to the introduction of manning standards and the designation of funds for several positions as the main means of raising the quality of child protection. I agree that good working conditions and sufficient capacity are necessary to enable employees to do a good job. At the same time, I have been sceptical about solutions that involve strong detail management. I believe it is important to point out that it is the individual municipality that is responsible for child protection. I want the municipalities to take their responsibilities. They must also have the freedom to govern and organise child protection according to local circumstances and needs. Local government must be close to its own child protection service and follow up both quality and capacity. Municipalities must also see child protection in the context of other services providing family support and parental guidance. It is important that the manning needs are assessed locally by those who know where the shoe is pressing. Several measures have been taken by the government to increase awareness and knowledge of child protection in municipalities, so that priorities are good. We have provided network solutions that allow municipalities to access key data on their own child protection service and the ability to compare with other municipalities. The community has conducted dialogue meetings with the management of all municipalities to discuss the situation and staffing of child protection services, and we have submitted proposals to the Stortinget to introduce annual status reporting requirements to the local government. Since 2013, job growth has been significant, and this is due to the fact that municipalities give greater priority to child protection. That's good. I will continue to follow the manning situation and facilitate positive development. It has been repeatedly pointed out that the quality of professional assessments and justifications varies too much. Often, careful assessments of children’s needs and the kind of help they need are lacking. High demands must be made on the skills and discretion of employees. Employees themselves have also called for more competence. Therefore, empowerment has been one of my most important priorities. We have sent proposals on competence requirements for consultation. Here we propose, among other things: requirements for master’s degree for managers and for those performing core tasks in municipal child protection. This will mean a significant lifting. More knowledge will make employees better equipped to solve their problems. Higher skills requirements will also contribute to increased status and make child protection more attractive as a workplace for skilled professionals. New skills requirements are combined with measures to strengthen child protection training. We have also provided a strong improvement in the provision of continuing training for those who already work in the services. Over 90 million. kr is allocated to capacity-building actions in 2020. It is an important objective to increase confidence in child protection in families with a minority background. There is therefore a strong emphasis on increasing the ability of employees to relate and to understand culture. An important objective is that, through good and trusting cooperation with families, child protection services should be able to find relief measures that families experience as useful and valuable. Good action and early help will help to reduce the need for care provision. One of the competence measures in this context is a new further training offer at the VID Science College in Stavanger, which started in the autumn of 2019. There has also been a development of a provision of training in minority competences that will be offered to selected municipalities and establishments in the state child protection sector in the spring of 2020. In a new regulation on learning outcomes in child protection education, a bachelor's degree in child protection, there is, among other things: clarification of the knowledge of the life situation and rights of indigenous, ethnic and refugees. We also assess how cultural bridge builders, or link workers, can help strengthen trust and interaction between public services and users. Several other measures are in place to improve the trust of minority families in the service. Several of these are part of the Bufdir Action Plan to improve trust between ethnic minority communities and child protection 2016-2021. The plan includes, inter alia: measures to further develop the competence of the Child Protection Service, increase user involvement, improve the use of interpreters and inform about the work of child protection. In 2020, around 11 million were also allocated. kr to strengthen the relief work of child protection. The resources shall make the relief work more systematic and adapted to individual needs. Testing of the new model starts in the autumn. At the same time, we are working to further develop the content of Bufetat's specialised actions for the families of infants and young children and to assist children and young people in need of comprehensive follow-up. We're also doing a great job of giving municipal child protection a very desirable digital boost. Today, child protection does not make good use of the opportunities offered by digital tools and solutions. This now makes Buffdir and KS something in common with, through the DigiBarn Protection initiative. The government has set a total of 30 million. to DigiBarnevern in 2020. The first delivery from DigiBarnevern is the new digital portal for anxiety messages. The portal was opened in April and allows a child’s concern to be notified digitally, in a simple and secure way. Digital concerns messages can enhance the legal certainty of children by speeding up the delivery of messages and providing more relevant content, and lowering the threshold for reporting concerns. Several digital solutions will be launched in the period up to 2023. Developments include: solutions for the collection of data for statistical purposes and solutions for the secure exchange of electronic messages between services. This simplifies work processes and ensures better management data. With more efficient working processes and better professional support integrated into the ICT system, employees will be given more time to follow up on children and families. Good governance data are important for the state and municipalities in further developing child protection. The quality of the offer needs to be improved, and we need to have less undesired variation between municipalities. Better health care for children in child protection is a high priority of the government. Children in child protection are not adequately addressed in their health care needs. The provision of services is often not adapted to the fact that many children need care from child protection and health care at the same time. We have already taken a number of measures in this area. The aim is to monitor children and young people in the field of child protection and to provide rapid health care. Bufdir and the Health Directorate have recently completed an investigation into the health monitoring of children in child protection. In the Buffetat region south and north, a new institutional service is also being tested for children who both need long-term care away from home and a high need for health care. It is developed in close cooperation between health businesses and Bufetat. Some children and young people are so violent and criminal that they must be protected from themselves. Sometimes society must also be protected against them. These are the children and young people who have not received the help they needed in their childhood. The bigger their challenges have been, the harder it is to get in the position to help them. Many of these have themselves been subjected to violence, abuse or care failure. Good help requires joint efforts by several agencies. At the same time, we must be honest that children who commit serious and repeated crime challenge society and our legal system. It's hard to help these kids, and we haven't done enough so far. The government works across sectors to get a targeted and comprehensive effort in this area. We gain more knowledge about what works. We will then assess the need for change, so that children and young people who commit criminal acts are given the best possible help. The government has earmarked 10 million. kr to strengthen child protection efforts in Oslo targeting children aged 12 to 15 who commit or are at risk of crime. The funds go to measures aimed at helping children out of criminal environments and also contribute to closer cooperation between child protection and the police. Child protection has a responsibility to help children and young people who commit serious or repeated crime. However, it is important that child protection measures be taken on the basis of the child’s need for care and treatment, taking into account the best interests of the individual child. Measures justified by societal need for protection are not part of the mandate of child protection. Our policy is based on the fact that the best thing for children is to grow up in a family. Therefore, foster homes are also the most commonly used option for children who are unable to live at home. Of children and young people with child protection, 60 were received by the end of 2018. home help measures. About 80 pst. children and young people who are unable to live at home live in foster homes. It is difficult to recruit sufficient foster homes to meet the needs of different children. It is also a challenge that too many foster homes end up broken. My goal is for all children to have a safe and stable home. My main task here is to strengthen municipal foster home work. We can get more and more foster homes in children's families and networks. Here there has been a positive trend since 2013, but there are still too many differences between municipalities. Therefore, we now put more pressure on this work through guidance and other follow-up of the municipalities. I also want to mobilise non-governmental organisations in a joint effort to provide more foster homes. The strengthening of municipal foster home work is also about guidance and follow-up. Good support is often essential for foster families to cope with the task. It's set at $25 million. kr for the development of community guidance and follow-up services for foster homes. The funds are used for cooperation projects between municipalities. The community has a central role to play in following up the development work in the municipalities. This work continues in the years to come. We will also work to recruit foster homes that can take care of children's language, religion and culture in line with the obligations of the Child Protection Act. However, the position of the child will depend on what is best for the individual child. We are now working on following up the proposals from the Foster Home Committee, which delivered its NOU in 2018. We consider whether there should be clearer requirements for foster home work in regulations. Bufdir is well in the process of developing professional tools to help both municipalities and Bufetat. We are also working to develop clearer criteria for the remuneration and free purchase of foster homes, so that it is easier for both the municipalities and foster parents to know what conditions apply. Not all children can get the help they need in a family. For some children, a stay in a child-care facility is the best offer for a period of time. Often these are children and young people with serious behavioural problems, mental or drug problems. Despite heavy baggage and major challenges, all children have resources, opinions, dreams and opportunities. Employees of child protection institutions shall contribute to the development of children living there, expressed their opinions, realized their dreams and laid a good foundation for standing firm in their own lives. Institutions shall provide for safety, sound care and treatment and ensure that the legal certainty of children is safeguarded during their stay. Employees are charged with a large and ongoing responsibility for children and young people in a very vulnerable situation. This places great demands on the quality and professionalism of the individual. Institutional child protection has many highly skilled employees, but also a large proportion of unskilled workers. In proposals for new skills requirements, which we have now consulted, we propose that those employed in a child protection institution should have a relevant bachelor's degree and that the head of institution should have a relevant master's degree. Institutions report an increase in the number of children with complex needs. They often need tailor-made measures and high staffing around them. There's some background for strengthening the budget of Bufatat in recent years, and for the growth of the only measures we've seen in recent years. I assume that when single measures are chosen, it is based on the best interests of the child. However, provision must be made for children to live as far as possible with other children. Therefore, Bufdir is mandated to review the use of exclusive measures. The provision of institutions is an area of action where quality and legal certainty are particularly important. I am therefore pleased that we have a comprehensive and thorough supervision system that helps to point out offences, errors, weaknesses and deficiencies. This is important for our improvement work in this area. We are constantly working on strengthening the quality of the institutional service, but at the same time we have to recognise that this is a demanding job. Institutional child protection is at the heart of public the exercise of powers. The law opens up the use of coercion and power against children in a particularly vulnerable situation. The risk of failure is therefore high. The area is complex, with interaction challenges and a risk of unclear responsibilities between different services. This concerns, in particular, healthcare and criminal services. These are the challenges I am committed to finding good solutions to, together with the Minister of Health and the Minister of Justice. It is absolutely necessary that we work thoroughly and knowledge-based, and that decisions are made on a sound professional basis. Both the Department and the Directorate have launched research and investigations concerning the protection of the institutional child. I cannot rule out the need to take action to further strengthen the knowledge base. Private actors make a significant contribution to both municipal and state child protection. We need to be sure that they provide good and cost-effective services. The government has therefore sent to the consultation a proposal to amend the regulation of private actors in the protection of children, with a view to legislative changes from 2022. Here we propose, among other things: to limit the right of child protection to be assisted by private operators in tasks involving the exercise of public authority. To contribute to the diversity of supply, I want to strengthen the position of ideal suppliers. This is also central to the government platform and is the background for continuing the cooperation agreement with non-profit providers on health and social services in 2019. The agreement recognises, makes visible and strengthens the role of the ideal actors. We have also established new framework agreements for non-profit centres for parents and children, and Bufdir has been given the task of designing a strategy for implementing the Stortingen request decision to increase the proportion of non-profit institutions serving households. Bufdir has also been asked to give priority to non-profit actors over commercial ones where it can be done. I know that many burns to help strengthen child protection. We see it through the employees who every day are on the verge of giving children and young people the best possible growth. We also see it through a non-profit sector that is clearly present in child protection, and through contributions from many others that every day make an effort in child protection. I am both happy and grateful for that. I'm glad that so many people are concerned about and show a great commitment to child protection. I am also pleased that we have a supervisory system that helps to focus attention on risk areas. This is important for our continuous improvement work. As one of the most important welfare services for children and young people, critical attention to the work being done is essential. We have to endure criticism, and we have to use it constructively to create even better child protection. Not least, we need to listen to the experience of those who receive assistance from child protection. User organisations have an important role to play here. Child protection is doing a lot of good work. We just don't hear much about it. These are not the things that end well, which are illuminated in the EMD. ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e5045	I would like to thank the Cooperation Committee, but I also want to thank someone else. The cleaning service at Stortinget has done a great job all day, and since it is optional which speaker we are using, I thought that we in the committee could exchange, so that there will be a little less stress and a little more efficient implementation of the meeting. It is now almost 20 years since the marriage pension was converted, from public service pension to a simpler regulation where the pension is calculated independently of benefits from popular security. But long-term transitional rules still make it possible to receive a spouse pension in accordance with the old rules. The rule for the coordination of gross spouse pension from public service pension is one of the most complex legislation of the pension system. It would be even more complicated if the coordination rules were also to be adapted to the phase-in of a new age pension from the population gap from 1954 onwards. To avoid this, the government has proposed a faster phase-in of net pension than proposed 20 years ago. It will be difficult to design coordination rules against new age pension from public interest with the same characteristics as current coordination rules. For this reason, persons entitled to a gross spouse pension who partially earn an old-age pension under new vesting rules will receive pension levels other than those of older age. There has been a general consensus that it would be unfortunate to complicate the legislation in order to calculate spouse pensions with a new set of complex coordination rules. Several hearing bodies recognise that it is a paradox that the non-tested benefits provide the largest payment to surviving spouses who have their own income and self-earned occupational pension. Nevertheless, organisations wish to increase the originally proposed rate for non-needed spouse pensioners at triple normal rates. Most hearing bodies have supported the Ministry's assessment of replacing the gross performance with a net estimated performance of 67 years and have justified this in terms of making understandable rules, legal certainty and predictability for pensioners. There is an overall committee behind the vote, and I also thank the Committee on Cooperation in the case.
ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e5058	Thank you to the rapporteur, who has dropped the committee through a complicated case, and who has ended up with a unanimous vote in line with the government's proposal. As stated in the vote and also referred to by the rapporteur: «The rules for the coordination of the gross spouse pension from public service pension are one of the most complex legislation of the pension scheme.» And as if that were not enough, the legislation would be further complicated with another set of coordination rules between the gross widow's pension and the new retirement pension from social security – in part, for those born between 1954 and 1962, and completely, for those born from 1963 and later. For all those who have followed the years of debates on ‘gold widows’, ‘zero widows’, legislative changes with or without retroactive effect — and in the middle of it all — a European Union index on men’s and women’s equal rights, today’s proposal for simplification must come as a relief. Hopefully, this also applies to future beneficiaries of a spouse pension from a public service pension. They can have a certain hope of understanding what they get in the total pension, and why. As the rapporteur has explained, the proposal is aimed at short-term proceedings and replacing gross spouse pension, probationary or not, with a net imputed spouse pension from 67 years of age, phased in gradually with a new age pension from popular security for those born from 1954 onwards. The fact that the net spouse pension was actually introduced for the vast majority of people already in 2001 also promotes the absence of new, complex coordination rules for a numerically small group, which also have two sets of coordination rules at present, more favourable to those with non-necessary tests than to those with a need-tested spouse pension. With the conversion to a net spouse pension, we do not create new rules for the coordination of two types of gross spouse pension with a new population pension, and we do not create new rules for the shortening of the gross spouse pension for those who themselves have an old-age pension from a public occupational pension. It is also worth noting that employee organisations support the proposal submitted in a joint opinion. The proposal for two different rates, a high rate for those who had a non-need-tested gross right, and a low rate for those who had a need-tested gross right, at the same time takes care of what has been a problem in the past when trying to simplify the legislation on spouse pension – i.e. constitutionally protected, already acquired rights. ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e5116	Although there is an overall committee which agrees on the importance of clarifying the rules and obligations of the Law on People's Rights to international agreements, it is nevertheless a shared position. The majority are in favour of adopting a proposal that the government should return to the Stortinget with proposals for regulatory audits now, while the minority in the Committee will reject this, as we believe it should be included in the work of a notified legislative committee, which is to look at Norwegian social security legislation against the EEA legislation. However, there is an overall committee which in any case believes that the mandate of such a legislative committee should also look at other international agreements. The Committee considers it important that ordinary people should be able to inform themselves of their rights and duties and that the State should also be obliged to facilitate them. Not least, we see this after we realized in the autumn of 2019 that we were facing a historical scandal, where the rules of popular fear have been mispracticed for a number of years. Although Nav has followed up the Storting's intentions in the People's Security Act, an interpretation error caused people to be subjected to a mispractice that has had a major impact on the individual. When social security lawyers have not even been able to interpret the law correctly, it is too much to demand that others be able to do more about their rights and duties. Not that it would have helped in this context, as long as fault practice was both systematic and systemic throughout all instances. We can't have it like that, and that's exactly what the committee agrees on. The Committee notes that Norway is bound by a number of international instruments regulating social security coordination, i.e. how to address the challenges of social security that may arise if a person has lived and/or worked in more than one country. The Committee considers that there is reason to consider whether this should be reflected in national legislation to a greater extent than at present, in the interests of both private individuals and professional users. For most people, however, the most important thing is not the text of the law itself, but that their rights and obligations are easily accessible and understandable. That is why the minority in the Committee believes that the proposal to come back with a new statutory audit does not now fully take place. However, the Committee points out that the government has already announced that they will set up a legislative committee to look at Norwegian social security legislation against EEA legislation. The mandate of such a legislative selection should require the sample to look at the relationship between Norwegian social security legislation and other agreements concluded by Norway with a bearing on social security coordination. Of course, it is expected that the case will be submitted to Stortinget in an appropriate manner once the law commission has provided its investigation.

At the same time, as the Committee also points out, it must be weighed against the obligation of Norway under the EEA Agreement to implement Regulations ‘as such’ in Norwegian law, which means that, as a general rule, regulations are to be implemented by means of so-called incorporation. This means that after the legislative committee has delivered its inquiry, and any proposals have been at regular consultation, the matter will be submitted to the Stortinget in an appropriate manner.

More parties than those who formed a majority at the time were in favour of regional reform with larger units than the former county municipalities, but there was no willingness to wide-ranging conciliation, despite repeated invitations. I can confirm that myself. It was primarily about the Workers' Party and the Center Party, which has seen the most useful fishing in angry water both then and later. Regional reform shifts power to popular regional political governance. Through regional discretion, more adapted solutions are provided, the funds are used more accurately, and it is not certain that what was tricked in Troms and Finnmark is the same as has been tricked in Agder. From the very beginning, it was said that the reform was a structural change that was to be further developed. This work has now started, and more tasks need to be put in place. The government has invested large sums of money in the reform, and thousands of employees have shown great ability to adapt and have a new employer and a new place of work. Putting processes back into game now will be irresponsible simply.
ParlaMint-NO_2020-05-26.ana.ud289e5519	After several articles where costs are mentioned, it is tempting, when you look beyond the glass-ceilings, to say that yes, democracy costs. Regional reform has been discussed and dealt with several times in this Chamber. On 1 January this year, the reform became a fact. It's now eleven counties in the country. But the reform has not even worked for five months until it is again up to discussion. Regional reform is about how to organise our regionally elected level in a way that provides the best possible services for citizens and businesses, and that provides opportunities for growth and development across the country. The counties have gained greater professional environments and can now pool their resources and competences to increase their capacity. The counties are thus even better equipped to provide good services to residents and businesses. We have transferred tasks and instruments to county councils in key areas: transport, competence, integration, industry, environment, public health and agriculture. These are important areas where county municipalities already have a lot of responsibility, and which the counties can now see more in context. In this way the county councils become stronger community developers. Today, the Stortinget deals with two representative proposals – or, in fact, many more than that – but in this particular case two representative proposals: one that the government should establish mechanisms for abolishing county mergers for the states that want to do so, and one that would end the county to cut red tape and bring services closer to the population. We already have a mechanism on the question of the division of states – the law of division. The division law has the necessary rules on the process of changing borders between municipalities and counties. It follows from the law of division that the Grand Duchy decides on the aggregation and division of counties, and it is the Grand Duchy that must adopt any changes in the state structure. It also follows from the law that the counties may promote applications for the initiation of a divisional inquiry. If the Ministry receives such an application, the Ministry will ensure that matters relating to the department's assessment and recommendation are promoted for the Stortinget. I believe it is important to respect the decisions taken in relation to regional reform. Extensive efforts have been made in the counties to follow up the decision of the Grand Duchy on county mergers. The winding-up of the county as a administrative body will have a major impact on the division of tasks in Norway. As there are still many small municipalities in Norway and the generalist municipality principle is underpinned, there are restrictions on which tasks can be transferred to the municipalities. The county councils are currently responsible for many major tasks, and from 2020 they have been responsible for even more tasks and instruments. In the event of a closure of the county level, more tasks will have to be transferred to the state, not the municipalities, and thus the task solution will be placed even further from the inhabitants than today. It is time we set the finish line for the re-commitment of majority decisions in the Grand Duchy on the merger of county councils. Then there will be a time for evaluation and assessment of whether we have achieved the objectives of the regional reform. Until then, I believe that we should all base the new state structure, look ahead and use the forces to ensure that everyone has access to good services regardless of where they live. Only then will the county councils be able to develop the county for the benefit of citizens and businesses.
